You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Original LSAT U teaching and exercises for Joshua\'s review. This file does not publish, assign or change platform content.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Give the teacher two independently assignable choices\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Learn and practice Group 1:** the explanation, worked examples, guided attempts, selected existing practice and fresh independent practice below.\\r\\n2. **Practice Group 1:** the standalone drills below, with the teaching available as a reference. Beginner, Medium and Advanced are separate sets within the drill.\\r\\n\\r\\nExisting Activities remain reusable. These eighteen fresh items supplement them rather than replace their content. Difficulty describes interpretation and reasoning demand. The recommended starting format is untimed. Optional Medium and Advanced timing is confirmed, but timing defaults, exact limits and readiness requirements are not approved. Beginning with accurate untimed work is a teaching recommendation, not an advancement gate or a prohibition on Beginner timing.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is a reviewed starting set, not exhaustive coverage of every Group 1 expression. Positive conditional uses of when and where, further the-only contrasts, and sufficient fresh items per expression still need item-level tagging or expansion before the package can be declared complete.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Hint behavior is proposed, pending Joshua\'s reply:** each item has one optional, specific hint requested by the student. Keep it separate from the answer. Record whether it was used so a supported response is distinguishable from an independent one. This draft does not prescribe scoring penalties, advancement percentages or automatic mastery thresholds.\\r\\n\\r\\n## A guarantee gives you a direction\\r\\n\\r\\nIf a storage unit\'s alarm is armed, its status light is illuminated.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe sufficient condition is **the storage unit\'s alarm is armed**. That is enough to guarantee **its status light is illuminated**, the necessary condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Alarm armed → status light illuminated.**\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule does not say that an alarm is armed now. It tells you what follows if an alarm is armed. A separate fact, such as “Unit 12\'s alarm is armed,” lets you apply the rule to Unit 12. You can then conclude that Unit 12\'s status light is illuminated.\\r\\n\\r\\nAn illuminated light does not prove an armed alarm. The light could be illuminated in another way. An unarmed alarm does not prove an unilluminated light either. The rule gives no outcome for that starting situation. Both conclusions would add a direction the author did not give you.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the rule **alarm armed → light illuminated**, compare these fully described cases:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Case | Does it fit the rule? | Why |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Alarm armed; light illuminated | Allowed | The stated guarantee is satisfied. |\\r\\n| Alarm armed; light not illuminated | Forbidden | The sufficient condition occurs without its required result. |\\r\\n| Alarm not armed; light illuminated | Allowed | The rule does not prohibit other ways to illuminate the light. |\\r\\n| Alarm not armed; light not illuminated | Allowed | The rule makes no positive demand when its trigger is absent. |\\r\\n\\r\\n“Allowed” means consistent with the rule. It does not mean the case actually happened. When judging a conclusion from supplied information, use **Guaranteed**, **Ruled out** or **Unknown**. Unknown means the conclusion could be true or false while the supplied information stays true. This avoids treating “possible” as “proven.”\\r\\n\\r\\n## Translate complete ideas before shortening them\\r\\n\\r\\nUse these four steps:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Identify the phrase expressing the guarantee.\\r\\n2. Write the complete condition that is sufficient. Include its subject, action, negatives and relevant modifiers.\\r\\n3. Write the complete condition that follows. Keep the same person or object when the sentence concerns one subject.\\r\\n4. Write **sufficient condition → necessary condition**, then read it as an ordinary if-then sentence. Compare that sentence with the original.\\r\\n\\r\\nShort labels come after those complete ideas. For example, “a delivery vehicle is registered with the harbor office” may become **registered with harbor office**. It should not become just **vehicle**, because that loses the condition you need to satisfy.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Worked example: a universal statement has the same direction\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery delivery vehicle registered with the harbor office displays a numbered permit.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe sufficient condition is **a delivery vehicle is registered with the harbor office**. The necessary condition is **that vehicle displays a numbered permit**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Delivery vehicle registered with harbor office → displays numbered permit.**\\r\\n\\r\\nRead it back: if a delivery vehicle is registered with the harbor office, that vehicle displays a numbered permit. The complete subject survives. The statement does not say that every delivery vehicle displays a permit. It also does not say that every vehicle displaying a permit is registered with that office.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Guided attempt: clause order does not decide direction\\r\\n\\r\\nA status report is sent whenever a sensor restarts.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Which complete idea follows whenever?\\r\\n2. What does the statement guarantee from that idea?\\r\\n3. Write the arrow and read it back in English.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** the sufficient condition can appear second in the sentence. Start with the idea introduced by whenever.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Sensor restarts → status report sent.** Whenever the sensor restarts, a report is sent. A sent report alone does not establish that the sensor restarted.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Guided attempt: keep a modifier inside the subject\\r\\n\\r\\nAny membership application submitted through the evening service receives a confirmation number.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Is the subject every application, or a restricted group of applications?\\r\\n2. Write that complete sufficient condition.\\r\\n3. State what follows about that same application.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** include how the application was submitted before putting it left of the arrow.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Membership application submitted through evening service → receives confirmation number.** The rule gives no guarantee for an application submitted through another service.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Read the sentence before trusting an indicator list\\r\\n\\r\\nGroup 1 commonly includes if, whenever, when, where, every, all, any and the only. Guarantee verbs can express the same direction. A list helps you recognize familiar wording; the sentence determines whether a guarantee is present and what its conditions are.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **When** can report a single event: “When I arrived on Tuesday, the lights were off.” That sentence does not establish a general arrival-to-darkness rule.\\r\\n- **Where** can identify a location rather than a guarantee. Ask what the sentence actually commits the author to.\\r\\n- **If** can appear in an indirect question: “We asked if the certificate was valid.” That does not give a conditional rule.\\r\\n- **The only** needs careful parsing. “The only applications accepted are applications with a signed declaration” gives **application accepted → has signed declaration**. It does not make a declaration sufficient for acceptance. Do not assign a direction merely to the next isolated noun.\\r\\n\\r\\nGroup 2 develops requirement language such as only if and requires. Groups 3 and 4 develop translation that changes a condition\'s negation. This Group 1 package can compare directions, but it does not claim to teach those groups\' procedures. Negatives already written inside a Group 1 condition must still be preserved.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use harder Group 1 language after its prerequisites\\r\\n\\r\\nMedium adds longer predicates, clause placement, simple existing negation and contextual indicator checks. It does not require contraposition, compound connective rules or chains.\\r\\n\\r\\nAdvanced returns to sufficient-condition language with explicit dependencies:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A1:** joint AND antecedents. Teach first that both conditions are required together; one alone cannot trigger the rule.\\r\\n- **A2:** inclusive OR antecedents. Teach first that either branch can satisfy that antecedent; both are also permitted unless the statement excludes them.\\r\\n- **A3:** complete modifier scope. No AND/OR notation or contraposition required if the whole restricted subject remains one predicate.\\r\\n- **A4:** contraposition. Teach first that failure of the necessary condition excludes the sufficient condition. Keep the person and complete predicates consistent.\\r\\n- **A5:** rule versus existence. Teach first that a universal rule does not establish that any qualifying case exists.\\r\\n- **A6:** direct chaining and fact application. Teach first that a rule\'s result must satisfy the next rule\'s trigger. Linking rules alone does not establish a case; the supplied fact activates the chain here.\\r\\n\\r\\nAssign these items only after the named dependency has been taught. They are harder applications of Group 1 language, not a substitute for those lessons.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Beginner: six independent attempts\\r\\n\\r\\n### B1. Build the rule from every\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** Every inventory tag printed in orange is included in the daily inspection list. Write the complete sufficient and necessary conditions, then diagram the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Inventory tag printed in orange → included in daily inspection list.**\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** The guarantee applies to tags printed in orange. It does not apply to every inventory tag, and it does not tell you the color of other listed tags.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** include “printed in orange” in the left condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n### B2. Build the rule when if comes second\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** A parcel is released from the archive if its pickup receipt is signed. Diagram the rule without changing either predicate.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Parcel\'s pickup receipt signed → parcel released from archive.**\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** The if-clause identifies what is sufficient, even though it comes second. Release is the guaranteed result.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** begin with the receipt condition, not the first clause on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n### B3. Classify complete cases\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** If an access code is accepted, the green light is on. Mark each case Allowed or Forbidden: (a) accepted code, green light on; (b) accepted code, green light off; (c) code not accepted, green light on; (d) code not accepted, green light off.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** (a) Allowed; (b) Forbidden; (c) Allowed; (d) Allowed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Only (b) supplies the sufficient condition while denying its required result. The rule gives no required outcome when a code is not accepted. Allowed cases are possible, not necessarily actual.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** find the case where the trigger occurs but its result does not.\\r\\n\\r\\n### B4. Separate a result from proof of its trigger\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** Every document entered in the tracking register receives a reference number. Document R has received a reference number. Is “Document R was entered in the tracking register” Guaranteed, Ruled out or Unknown?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** Unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Entry guarantees a number. Having a number does not establish entry. Document R could have received its number through another process, or through this one.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** compare the supplied fact with the right side of your arrow.\\r\\n\\r\\n### B5. Repair an inference from an absent trigger\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** If a volunteer attends the briefing, the volunteer receives a printed guide. Neri did not attend the briefing. A student concludes, “Neri did not receive a printed guide.” Repair the student\'s conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** Whether Neri received a printed guide is Unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Attendance is one sufficient way to receive a guide. The rule does not prohibit giving a guide to someone who did not attend. Negating the sufficient condition does not establish a negated result.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** does the rule say attendance is the only way to receive a guide?\\r\\n\\r\\n### B6. Apply the rule to its matching fact\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** Any subscriber whose account is active receives the weekly digest. Bela is a subscriber whose account is active. State the guaranteed conclusion about Bela and identify why it follows.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** Bela receives the weekly digest.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Bela satisfies the complete sufficient condition: subscriber with an active account. Apply the guarantee to that same person. The rule by itself would not establish that Bela\'s account is active; the separate fact does.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** match Bela\'s fact to the entire subject described by any.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Medium: six attempts with more demanding language\\r\\n\\r\\n### M1. Preserve the restricted subject\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** Every research report using confidential survey data undergoes an ethics review. Report S uses public survey data; it uses no confidential survey data. Is “Report S undergoes an ethics review” Guaranteed, Ruled out or Unknown? Write the rule before answering.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Research report using confidential survey data → undergoes ethics review.** The conclusion is Unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Report S does not satisfy this trigger. That fails to prove a review, but it also fails to rule a review out. Another policy could require one.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** do not shorten the left side to “research report.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### M2. Interpret the only as a complete sentence\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** The only records accepted by the licensing office are digitally signed records. Diagram the relationship. Does a digital signature alone prove acceptance?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Record accepted by licensing office → digitally signed.** A digital signature alone does not prove acceptance.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** The sentence restricts the accepted records to digitally signed records. It does not say every signed record is accepted.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** start with a record that the office has accepted. What must be true of it?\\r\\n\\r\\n### M3. Keep the deadline inside the predicate\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** A completion certificate is emailed whenever a participant finishes the course before registration closes. Diagram the rule. Davi finishes the course after registration closes. Does the rule guarantee that Davi will not be emailed a completion certificate?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Participant finishes course before registration closes → participant emailed completion certificate.** No; the proposed negative conclusion is unsupported.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Finishing before registration closes is sufficient for an emailed certificate. Davi does not satisfy that complete trigger. The rule does not prohibit a certificate being emailed to someone who finishes later.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** include the deadline in the sufficient condition before matching Davi\'s fact.\\r\\n\\r\\n### M4. Preserve a negative already in the trigger\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** If a permit has not expired, its holder may enter the exhibit. The permit held by Ivo has not expired. What follows? Does the rule also tell you what follows for an expired permit?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** Ivo may enter the exhibit. For an expired permit, permission to enter is Unknown from this rule alone.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** The complete sufficient condition includes not expired. Ivo meets it. An expired permit negates that trigger, which does not prove lack of permission. “May enter” means permission, not proof of actual entry.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** distinguish permission to enter from the act of entering.\\r\\n\\r\\n### M5. Repair the direction of a guarantee verb\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** The policy says that completing the identity check guarantees issuance of a visitor pass. A student diagrams **Visitor pass issued → identity check completed**. Repair the diagram and explain why the first version added information.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Identity check completed → visitor pass issued.**\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Completing the check is sufficient for issuance. The reversed arrow would claim that every issued pass proves a completed check, excluding other possible ways to obtain a pass that this policy does not exclude.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** ask which event the policy says is enough for the other event.\\r\\n\\r\\n### M6. Distinguish a past event from a general guarantee\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** (a) When I visited the records room on Monday, its service window was closed. (b) Whenever the records room\'s alarm is being tested, its service window is closed. Which sentence supplies a general conditional rule? Diagram that rule. Does it connect visits with closures?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** Sentence (b): **Records room\'s alarm being tested → its service window closed.** It does not connect visits with closures.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** Sentence (a) reports what happened during one visit. Sentence (b) guarantees closure under the stated testing condition. The occurrence of when in a sentence does not automatically establish a universal rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** look for a commitment about every qualifying occasion, rather than one dated occasion.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Advanced: six attempts after the named prerequisites\\r\\n\\r\\n### A1. Retain a joint trigger\\r\\n\\r\\n**Prerequisite:** AND antecedents.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** If a proposal is filed before noon and carries the supervisor\'s signature, it enters today\'s review queue. Proposal T was filed before noon. Is “Proposal T enters today\'s review queue” Guaranteed, Ruled out or Unknown? Explain which condition is missing from the supplied facts.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** Unknown. Whether Proposal T carries the supervisor\'s signature is not supplied.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** **Filed before noon AND carries supervisor\'s signature → enters today\'s queue.** The fact establishes only one part of the joint trigger. It does not establish the guarantee\'s prerequisites together. This also does not rule out queue entry through another rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** underline both conditions inside the if-clause before matching the fact.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A2. Use either sufficient branch without adding exclusivity\\r\\n\\r\\n**Prerequisite:** inclusive OR antecedents.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** Any consignment arriving by rail or by ferry receives a transit code. Consignment U arrives by ferry. State what is guaranteed. Does the rule prohibit U from also arriving by rail as part of its journey?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** U receives a transit code. The rule does not prohibit it from also arriving by rail.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Explanation:** **Arrives by rail OR arrives by ferry → receives transit code.** The ferry branch satisfies the sufficient condition. Inclusive or allows either branch or both; the sentence gives no exclusivity requirement.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional hint:** one stated alternative can satisfy an inclusive-or trigger. Do not add “but not both.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### A3. Keep two modifiers attached to the same object\\r\\n\\r\\n**Prerequisite:** complete modifier scope; no compound notation required.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Task:** Every report released outside the agency without redactions receives a public-disclosure audit. Report V is released outside the agency, but it contains redactions. A student shortens the rule to **Released outside agency → receives audit**. Repair the shortened condition and judge whether V\'s audit is guaranteed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Answer:** **Report released outsid…3604 tokens truncated…y merely because it already exists.\\r\\n- Remove the confusing “Think before you turn” framing and the corner “Explain in own words” / “Turn explanation” controls from this practice presentation.\\r\\n- Resolve the ambiguous “Revisit card” action. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 8.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"7f3c76","wall_time_seconds":0.3961031,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":5935,"output":"        <div className=\\"co-role-pair\\"><div><h3>Sufficient</h3><p>Win the raffle: enough to establish getting a bicycle.</p><strong>Goes left</strong></div><div><h3>Necessary</h3><p>Get a bicycle: required whenever you win.</p><strong>Goes right</strong></div></div>\\r\\n        <p>Now compare what each starting fact tells us. Keep the same rule while you switch between winning and getting a bicycle.</p>\\r\\n        <GateExplorer/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Having a requirement does not guarantee the result\\">\\r\\n        <p>Let\'s use a different requirement.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Entering the archive requires a pass.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>The sentence tells us what someone needs for entry: a pass. If someone enters the archive, they have that pass. That\'s why has a pass belongs on the required side.</p>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Enters the archive\\" right=\\"Has a pass\\"/>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Mina has a pass. Does this sentence establish that she entered the archive?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Mina could have a pass and remain outside. The rule tells us what entry requires; it doesn\'t tell us that every person with a pass enters.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Necessary doesn\'t mean enough. A requirement can be present while the other condition remains unknown. To conclude Mina entered, we\'d need information establishing that additional connection.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Every finalist receives an invitation. Which requirement does this sentence establish?\\" options={[\'Receiving an invitation is necessary for being a finalist.\', \'Being a finalist is necessary for receiving an invitation.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence gives an invitation to every finalist. That makes receiving one required for finalist status; it does not restrict invitations to finalists.\', \\"You reversed the requirement. Your choice would mean anyone receiving an invitation has to be a finalist. The sentence allows someone who isn\'t a finalist to receive an invitation too.\\"]}/>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n        <p>We can check a proposed arrow by imagining a case in which its left side is true. Read the original sentence and ask what you now have to know about that same case. Your answer should match the right side of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n        <p>This is a <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong>. Read the rule as if it concerns you or something of yours. The point is to test what the sentence guarantees, not guess what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n        <blockquote>If you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>For this rule, imagine yourself in the raffle. Keep the same raffle and the same bicycle outcome in mind on both sides.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If I win the raffle, what do I know?\\">\\r\\n          <p>I get a bicycle. The rule covers the case in which I win. Win the raffle → Get a bicycle matches what the sentence tells me.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"If I get a bicycle, must I have won the raffle?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. I could get a bicycle without winning by buying one or receiving a gift. That fits the rule because it does not create a winner who fails to get a bicycle.</p>\\r\\n          <p>Get a bicycle → Win the raffle would make every bicycle receipt proof of winning. The sentence does not give me that direction. Trying both readings lets me catch the reverse instead of accepting an arrow just because it looks familiar.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n      <Section title=\\"Read the roles in the relationship\\">\\r\\n        <p>In <strong>Win the raffle → Get a bicycle</strong>, the left condition is sufficient for the right: it is enough to establish it. The right condition is necessary for the left: it is required whenever the left holds.</p>\\r\\n        <p>These names describe roles in this rule. A different sentence could give the same idea a different role. Don\'t ask whether a pass is sufficient or necessary by itself. Ask what the complete sentence says having the pass does.</p>\\r\\n        <p>The next two groups give us ways to recognize these roles in varied wording. We\'ll keep returning to the meaning because an indicator can only help if you know what complete idea it refers to.</p>\\r\\n      </Section>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function GroupLessons({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  const group = GROUPS.find(item => item.id === (id === \'group-1\' ? \'g1\' : \'g2\'))!;\\r\\n  const first = id === \'group-1\';\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Find the sufficient idea\' : \'Find what is required\'}>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"A sufficient condition gives you enough information to establish the other condition. Group 1 helps you recognize that job in several kinds of sentence. For example, all registered guests covers the registered guests. It doesn\'t cover every guest who happens to be nearby. We\'ll check which people or situations each expression includes.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"A necessary condition is something the other condition requires. Group 2 helps us recognize what must be true when that other condition holds. The requirement belongs on the right because the left condition cannot hold without it. Having the requirement, though, leaves the left condition unknown unless something else settles it.\\"}</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-vocabulary\\">{group.words.map(word => <span key={word}>{word}</span>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>An <strong>indicator</strong> is an expression that helps us recognize the relationship. Its <strong>target</strong> is the complete idea it refers to. For example, in “All registered guests receive a wristband,” the target of all is registered guests. Leaving out registered would make the rule apply to guests it never covered.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Before looking for a word, ask what the sentence says. Does it give a rule, cover every member of a group, promise a result, or name something required? Each can tell us that if one condition holds, another must hold. A sentence about what usually happens leaves room for exceptions; a conditional rule makes its consequence certain whenever its sufficient condition holds.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read the arrow as <strong>must</strong>. The sufficient condition goes on the left. The necessary condition goes on the right. If the left condition is true, the right condition must be true too. The arrow doesn\'t say which event happened first, and it doesn\'t establish that one event caused the other.</p>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title={first ? \'Different wording can make the same rule\' : \'Read it as a requirement\'}>\\r\\n      {first ? <>\\r\\n        <p>Suppose a studio has this rule: every painting with a silver seal has been inspected. Having the seal is enough to establish inspection. Let\'s keep that meaning and change the wording.</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>If a painting has a silver seal, it has been inspected.</blockquote><p>If what? It has a silver seal. That complete idea goes on the left.</p></div><div><blockquote>A painting has been inspected whenever it has a silver seal.</blockquote><p>Whenever what? It has a silver seal. The sufficient condition comes later in this sentence, but it still goes on the left.</p></div></div>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Has silver seal\\" right=\\"Has been inspected\\"/>\\r\\n        <p>The indicator can appear at the beginning or in the middle. Sentence position doesn\'t choose the side of the arrow. Find the idea the expression refers to, then ask what the sentence tells you if that idea is true.</p>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Each painting with a silver seal has been inspected. Did changing every to each change the rule?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Each still covers every painting with that seal. A painting with the seal that hadn\'t been inspected would break either statement. Keep has silver seal on the left and has been inspected on the right.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>Some wording presents a rule: <strong>if, when, whenever</strong>. Some covers a whole group: <strong>all, each, any, every</strong>. Read each in its sentence. Any in “Do you have any paint?” asks a question; it doesn\'t give us a rule.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Other sentences promise an outcome: “A silver seal ensures the painting has been inspected” or “A silver seal implies the painting has been inspected.” Here, the promised outcome is inspection. Ensures and implies express the arrow between the two complete ideas. Leads to, will, and always can also express a definite result in a rule, but the word alone doesn\'t settle the meaning. “The studio will open tomorrow” gives us no sufficient condition to put on the left.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Try the next sentences by imagining the condition each one covers. If you know a guest is registered, what follows? If you know a report arrived before noon, what follows? Each expression will help us find that starting condition.</p>\\r\\n      </> : <>\\r\\n        <p>A useful way to read the whole rule is: <strong>the left condition requires the right condition</strong>. Suppose borrowing a studio camera requires a current membership. What is required for borrowing? A current membership. That\'s the necessary condition.</p>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\"><div><blockquote>Borrowing a studio camera requires a current membership.</blockquote><p>Requires names what borrowing needs: a current membership. An expired membership would not meet that requirement.</p></div><div><blockquote>A current membership is required to borrow a studio camera.</blockquote><p>The same required idea now comes first. Moving it in the sentence hasn\'t changed what borrowing requires.</p></div></div>\\r\\n        <Arrow left=\\"Borrows studio camera\\" right=\\"Has current membership\\"/>\\r\\n        <ThinkPause prompt=\\"Mina has a current membership. Does this rule tell us she borrowed a camera?\\">\\r\\n          <p>No. Membership is something borrowing requires. Mina could have the membership and never borrow a camera. The rule tells us what we need if she borrows; having what she needs doesn\'t tell us she did.</p>\\r\\n        </ThinkPause>\\r\\n        <p>Notice the meaning shared by <strong>requires, necessitates, depends on,</strong> and <strong>relies on</strong> when they express a strict requirement. If borrowing depends on having a current membership, borrowing still requires membership. “Depends partly on” may describe an influence instead, so read the whole claim before treating it as a conditional.</p>\\r\\n        <p>You may also hear the requirement as “In order to borrow a camera, you must have a current membership.” The goal is borrowing; the required condition is membership. “Only people with a current membership borrow a camera” and “You borrow a camera only if you have a current membership” keep membership required. Neither tells us that every member borrows one.</p>\\r\\n        <Check prompt=\\"Using the recording booth depends on having a reservation. Putting use on the right would make a reservation enough to establish that someone used the booth, which the sentence never says.\', \'A reservation is required for using the booth, so it belongs on the right. Having a reservation still leaves open whether the person uses it.\']}/>\\r\\n        <p>You won\'t always see a familiar word. Find what the sentence says is required <strong>for the other condition</strong>. Then check that your arrow keeps that requirement on the right.</p>\\r\\n      </>}\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    {IndicatorSections({ id })}\\r\\n    <Section id=\\"condition-practice\\" title={first ? \'Which reports does this rule cover?\' : \'What kind of pass does entry require?\'}>\\r\\n      {first ? <>\\r\\n        <blockquote>A report is reviewed if it is submitted before noon.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>If what? If the report is submitted before noon. Keep before noon with submitted. A report arriving at four in the afternoon does not meet the condition this sentence gives us.</p>\\r\\n        <p>Now suppose the report does arrive before noon. We know it is reviewed. Submission before noon is enough to establish review, so that condition belongs on the left of our arrow.</p>\\r\\n      </> : <>\\r\\n        <blockquote>Entry requires a current pass.</blockquote>\\r\\n        <p>What does entry require? A current pass. Current matters: an expired pass would not meet the requirement. Requires refers to that complete requirement, even if we rewrite the sentence as “A current pass is required for entry.”</p>\\r\\n        <p>Suppose someone enters. Under this rule, that person has a current pass. That puts entry on the left and the required pass on the right. Someone with a current pass could still stay outside.</p>\\r\\n      </>}\\r\\n      <p>This is the <strong>What Test</strong>: ask what the expression refers to, and answer with the words that make the condition complete. All what? Registered guests. Only if what? A parent signs the consent form. Guest alone drops registration; a parent alone drops the signature.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Try that question on each sentence below. When your answer includes the complete condition, the sentence will highlight its words. Then show the diagram and ask what you would know if its left condition were true. The right condition should be exactly what the sentence lets you conclude.</p>\\r\\n      <TranslationLab id={id}/>\\r\\n    </Section>\\r\\n    <Section title=\\"Keep the whole condition\\" dark>\\r\\n      <blockquote>{first ? \'Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.\' : \'Entry requires a current pass.\'}</blockquote>\\r\\n      <p>{first\\r\\n        ? \\"Applicant alone isn\'t the condition. The sentence is about an applicant who submits late. Keep that restriction when you shorten the idea; otherwise your diagram would make a claim about on-time applicants too.\\"\\r\\n        : \\"A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. The only people admitted. The complete target is admitted people. ## assistant

**Reframe this page as a short applied lesson on finding complete conditions.** Its current identity as another indicator-word destination is redundant after the revised definition pages, arrow lesson, expanded Drill 1 and dedicated flashcards.

Original purpose, grounded in the plan:
- `curriculum-analysis.md:42,142`: identify complete conditions despite word order and modifiers; translate, restate and repair direction.
- `group-1-learning-and-drills.md`: preserve the entire subject/predicate, distinguish conditional from incidental wording, then use guided and fresh practice.
- `2026-10-03-astra-opening-spec.md:119`: explicitly combine repeated introductions, arrow/word-order material and duplicated methods; remove repeated recaps that add no reasoning task.

**Keep the genuinely new skills:**
1. Preserve qualifications: “registered guests,” not merely “guests”; “submitted before noon,” not merely “submitted.”
2. Recognize when familiar words do **not** state a rule, such as “We asked if…” or a question using “any.”
3. Parse a complete expression such as “the only people admitted,” rather than attaching direction to an isolated word.

**Condense or remove from this destination:**
- Repeated sufficient-condition definition and left/right explanation.
- Repeated wording/order walkthrough already addressed by Must arrow.
- Word-by-word selector as the lesson’s organizing structure.
- Embedded legacy recall deck now duplicated by dedicated flashcards, including rejected labels.

Current composition in `ConditionalOpeningLesson.tsx:797+` includes definition/indicator/arrow exposition, silver-seal comparison, expression sections, translation practice, whole-condition check, “the only” explanation/check, diagram builder, recall cards and equivalence check. Not all these sections necessarily render on this route; `IndicatorNote` determines composition.

Current `openingCoursePath.ts:16` describes Drill 1 as condition roles, arrow placement, indicator recognition and fact application. That now overlaps the old page’s introductory purpose. The next lesson earns its place only by clearly advancing to **accurate sentence interpretation and complete-condition selection**, followed by the existing sufficient-practice drill.

No files changed.

3 : 5);\\r\\n  const family = sections(source.props.children).find(child => child.type === WordFamily);\\r\\n  if (!family) throw new Error(`Missing approved ${group} supplementary family`);\\r\\n  return <details className=\\"co-practical-note\\"><summary>More expressions for the same relationship</summary>\\r\\n    <p>Use the stated rule: anyone who borrows a locker has a key. These sentences preserve that connection. They do not say every person with a key borrows a locker.</p>\\r\\n    {family}\\r\\n  </details>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// Only and only if both mark the requirement, from different places in the sentence.\\r\\nfunction OnlyAndOnlyIf() {\\r\\n  return <NoteSection title=\\"Only and only if can mark the same requirement\\">\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>Only</strong> [people with a key] borrow a locker.</p><p>A person borrows a locker <strong>only if</strong> [they have a key].</p><span>Only and only if identify the necessary condition here.</span></div>\\r\\n    <p>Both sentences say locker borrowers have a key. In the first, only is at the beginning. In the second, only if is in the middle. Having a key is required in both, so it goes right. Read only if as the whole expression; the if inside it isn\'t doing the job if does on its own.</p>\\r\\n    <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A person borrows a locker only if they have a key. Which condition goes on the right?\\" options={[\'Borrows a locker\', \'Has a key\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Borrowing is what has the requirement. Ask what a locker borrower must have: a key. Borrowing goes left; having a key goes right.\', \'Having a key is required for borrowing. Only if appears in the middle, but it still identifies the necessary condition.\']}/>\\r\\n  </NoteSection>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction IndicatorNote({ group }: { group: \'group-1\' | \'group-2\' }) {\\r\\n  const [selected, setSelected] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [opened, setOpened] = useState<number[]>([0]);\\r\\n  const [focusPanel, setFocusPanel] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const panels = useRef<(HTMLDivElement | null)[]>([]);\\r\\n  const id = useId();\\r\\n  const content = groupSections(group);\\r\\n  const expressions = content.slice(2, 9);\\r\\n  const names = expressions.map(section => section.props.title?.split(\':\')[0]);\\r\\n  useEffect(() => {\\r\\n    if (!focusPanel) return;\\r\\n    panels.current[selected]?.focus();\\r\\n    setFocusPanel(false);\\r\\n  }, [focusPanel, selected]);\\r\\n  const choose = (index: number, moveFocus = false) => {\\r\\n    setSelected(index);\\r\\n    setOpened(previous => previous.includes(index) ? previous : [...previous, index]);\\r\\n    setFocusPanel(moveFocus);\\r\\n  };\\r\\n  const last = selected === expressions.length - 1;\\r\\n  // Two introductions and the seven complete expression explanations belong\\r\\n  // together in one concept note, not nine separate navigation destinations.\\r\\n  return <>{content.slice(0, 2)}\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-word-guide\\">Work through all seven expressions. Each one does the same job in a different sentence, and each has its own trap to check. Use Next expression to go in order, or choose any expression to read it again.</p>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-word-tabs\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Choose an expression\\">{expressions.map((section, index) => <button type=\\"button\\" key={section.props.title} aria-pressed={selected === index} aria-controls={`${id}-${index}`} onClick={() => choose(index)}>{names[index]}{opened.includes(index) && <><span aria-hidden=\\"true\\"> ✓</span><span className=\\"co-sr-only\\">, opened</span></>}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\" role=\\"status\\">Expression {selected + 1} of {expressions.length} · {opened.length} of {expressions.length} opened</p>\\r\\n    {expressions.map((section, index) => <div key={section.props.title} id={`${id}-${index}`} hidden={selected !== index} tabIndex={-1} ref={element => { panels.current[index] = element; }}>{section}</div>)}\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\">{last\\r\\n      ? <p>That was the last of the seven expressions. Choose any of them to read it again, or recall them with the cards below.</p>\\r\\n      : <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"co-primary\\" onClick={() => choose(selected + 1, true)}>Next expression: {names[selected + 1]}</button>}</div>\\r\\n    {group === \'group-2\' && <OnlyAndOnlyIf/>}\\r\\n    <FamilySupplement group={group}/>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Recall the expressions\\"><p>Once you\'ve read the seven examples, use these cards to recall them. Explain the relationship before turning each card, then compare the complete ideas and the direction with your answer.</p><StudyDeck group={group}/></NoteSection>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// Teach placement and reading through the familiar relationship, then require a decision.\\r\\nfunction MustArrowNote() {\\r\\n  return <section className=\\"co-section cr-course-section ma-lesson\\" data-reference-source={8}>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-section-heading\\"><h2>Left: sufficient. Right: necessary.</h2></div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"cr-step-body\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ma-lead\\">The arrow points from the <strong>sufficient condition</strong> to the <strong>necessary condition</strong>.</p>\\r\\n      <figure className=\\"ma-example\\" aria-label=\\"If you are enrolled in law school, you must be a student\\">\\r\\n        <figcaption className=\\"ma-read-instruction\\">Read left to right: <strong>“If the left side is true, the right side must be true.”</strong></figcaption>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"ma-relationship\\">\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-condition\\"><span className=\\"ma-side\\"><small>Left side</small>Sufficient condition</span><strong>You are enrolled in law school</strong><span className=\\"ma-meaning\\">Enough to guarantee the right side</span></div>\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-arrow\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><span>must</span><svg viewBox=\\"0 0 100 32\\" fill=\\"none\\"><path d=\\"M3 16H93M77 3L94 16L77 29\\" stroke=\\"currentColor\\" strokeWidth=\\"5\\" strokeLinecap=\\"round\\" strokeLinejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg></div>\\r\\n          <div className=\\"ma-condition\\"><span className=\\"ma-side\\"><small>Right side</small>Necessary condition</span><strong>You are a student</strong><span className=\\"ma-meaning\\">Required when the left side is true</span></div>\\r\\n        </div>\\r\\n      </figure>\\r\\n      <p>Being enrolled in law school is <strong>enough</strong> to know you are a student. That is why enrollment goes on the <strong>left</strong>. Being a student is <strong>required</strong>, so it goes on the <strong>right</strong>.</p>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-placement\\" aria-label=\\"Place and read the conditions\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Place the conditions. Then read the arrow.</h3>\\r\\n        <p>Choose what belongs on each side, then use the same direction with a new rule.</p>\\r\\n        <p className=\\"ma-compact-note\\">Put the <strong>condition</strong> beside the arrow, not the indicator word. For example, write “you win the raffle,” not “if.”</p>\\r\\n        <MustArrowPractice />\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <section className=\\"ma-order\\" aria-label=\\"Two ways to say the same thing\\">\\r\\n        <h3>Two ways to say the same thing</h3>\\r\\n        <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>If</strong> you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.</p><p>You get a bicycle <strong>if</strong> you win the raffle.</p></div>\\r\\n        <p>Both mean: <strong>You win the raffle → You get a bicycle.</strong> Winning is sufficient, so it goes left, even when it comes last in the sentence.</p>\\r\\n      </section>\\r\\n      <p className=\\"ma-takeaway\\"><strong>The arrow guarantees the right side when the left side is true.</strong> Being a student alone does not prove that you are enrolled in law school.</p>\\r\\n      <details className=\\"cr-extension ma-reverse\\"><summary>Why can\'t we reverse it?</summary><div className=\\"ma-reverse-body\\"><p>A person could be a history student who is not enrolled in law school. That possibility fits our rule.</p><p>The arrow states a relationship. By itself, it does not tell us that either condition is true, or that one causes the other.</p></div></details>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </section>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// One translation process, checked four ways, instead of five restarted lessons.\\r\\nfunction WholeMeaningNote() {\\r\\n  const onlyComparison = groupSection(\'group-2\', \'Compare only with the only\');\\r\\n  const insideCheck = Children.toArray(reference(12).props.children);\\r\\n  return <>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check a translation four ways\\">\\r\\n      <p>A diagram can point the right way and still say the wrong thing. Each check below catches a different slip in the same translation process.</p>\\r\\n      <ol>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Target:</strong> did you find the whole idea the indicator refers to, wherever it sits?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Qualification:</strong> did your short label keep every restriction?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Direction:</strong> did a similar-looking expression trick you into the wrong side?</li>\\r\\n        <li><strong>Imagined case:</strong> does your arrow promise something the sentence leaves open?</li>\\r\\n      </ol>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 1: find the whole target, wherever it sits\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator\'s <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The target often comes after its indicator. But often isn\'t always. Try these sentences in order.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>[Having a key] is <strong>required</strong> for borrowing a locker.</p><p>Borrowing a locker <strong>requires</strong> [having a key].</p><span>Having a key is the requirement in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>The target comes before required in the first sentence and after requires in the second. Having a key still goes right. Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable target rule. Ask which complete idea the sentence calls required, then give that idea its role.</p>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 2: keep every qualification\\">\\r\\n      <p>Once you have the target, shorten it without losing what limits it. These two sentences test the same habit from opposite sides of the arrow.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"co-word-comparison\\">\\r\\n        <div><blockquote>Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.</blockquote><p>Applicant alone isn\'t the condition. The sentence is about an applicant who submits late. Drop that restriction and your diagram makes a claim about on-time applicants too.</p></div>\\r\\n        <div><blockquote>Entry requires a current pass.</blockquote><p>A current pass is required. Has a pass drops current and would include an expired pass. A shorter label should save writing, not change the requirement.</p></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Any applicant who submits late pays a fee. What goes left?\\" options={[\'Applicant\', \'Applicant submits late\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You dropped the late-submission restriction. Applicant alone would cover every applicant, including someone who submits on time. The sufficient idea is applicant submits late.\', \'This keeps the complete sufficient idea. The rule covers applicants who submit late, so keep that restriction with the applicant.\']}/>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Entry requires a current pass. What goes right?\\" options={[\'Has current pass\', \'Has any pass\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'This keeps the full requirement. Entry needs a current pass; an expired pass does not meet that requirement.\', \'You dropped current from the requirement. Has any pass would include an expired pass, which is not what the sentence requires.\']}/>\\r\\n    </NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 3: only and the only point to different targets\\">{onlyComparison.props.children}</NoteSection>\\r\\n    <NoteSection title=\\"Check 4: try the sentence from the inside\\">{insideCheck.slice(0, -1)}</NoteSection>\\r\\n  </>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst freshCases = [\\r\\n  { rule: \'If it rains on Saturday, the market moves into the town hall.\', questions: [\\r\\n    { prompt: \\"It\'s Friday. You\'ve read this rule and nothing else. Where will the market be on Saturday?\\", options: [\'In the town hall.\', \'Outdoors, as usual.\', \\"The rule alone doesn\'t settle it.\\"], correct: 2, feedback: [\'You treated the rule as a weather report. It tells you what follows if it rains. Nobody has told you that it will rain.\', \\"You assumed it won\'t rain. The rule hasn\'t told you that either, so the market\'s location is still open.\\", \\"The rule describes what would happen if it rains. It\'s a promise about a possible case, not a report that the case happened.\\"] },\\r\\n    { prompt: \\"Saturday arrives, and it\'s raining. Now where is the market?\\", options: [\'In the town hall.\', \'Still not settled.\'], correct: 0, feedback: [\'Now you have a fact, not just a rule. The rule covers a rainy Saturday, and that\'s what we have, so the market\'s location is settled.\\"] },\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"4b7334","wall_time_seconds":0.2728256,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2428,"output":"---\\r\\nname: bootcamp-builder\\r\\ndescription: Build or revise a repo-native LSAT U bootcamp using corpus sources, approved teaching preferences, and the Intro to LR benchmark. Use when Joshua asks to build a topic bootcamp, revise a bootcamp, or save bootcamp teaching preferences. Preserves scope approvals and carries corrections across sessions.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n## Local execution contract (2026-10-10)\\r\\n\\r\\nPreserve current curriculum sources and brainstorms checkpoints. Resolve sibling app root/current quality benchmark and installed parity helper. Absent authoritative quality reference blocks parity claim. Commit/push only when actual task authorizes it, not merely because build is green.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Start with Joshua\'s accumulated preferences\\r\\n\\r\\nRead `references/teaching-preferences.md` beside this skill before every build or revision. It records confirmed corrections, exact approved wording, conditional-logic acceptance checks, and proposals that are not approved. Apply general standards across topics; use topic-specific examples only where they fit.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen Joshua requests an ongoing preference capture, maintain this same reference as corrections arrive. Do not create a competing master skill. Record date, scope, status (explicit correction / approved wording / provisional implementation), evidence, and the regression check. Update the owning catalog and project activation copies deliberately; preserve runtime-specific differences. Read this record at session pickup. A source-backed logical fact and a user-approved design are different kinds of evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a previously authorized revision, work within its scope without asking for every gate again. A request to review together remains a review until implementation is authorized.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Workflow\\r\\n\\r\\n# Bootcamp Builder\\r\\n\\r\\nTurn a topic + content into a shipped, repo-native LSAT U bootcamp that matches Intro to LR quality. This is the productized version of the Intro to LR (Main Conclusion) build process. It replaces `lsat-u-topic-builder`.\\r\\n\\r\\n**The template is the code, not a doc:** resolve the vault root from its `AGENTS.md`, then follow its application routing to `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/src/bootcamps/main-conclusion/` (the Intro to LR build). Read the app\'s own instructions and the template before scaffolding anything. If inspection needs a preserved feature checkout, record its path and commit and reconcile it against the current checkout before treating it as the implementation template. Its structure:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nsrc/bootcamps/{slug}/\\r\\n  App.tsx, routes.tsx        # nested <BootcampRoutes/> mounted under /bootcamp/{slug}/*\\r\\n  components/                # workspace-shell (chrome), drills, pickers, coach\'s note, command palette, trap-master, smart-hints...\\r\\n  content/*.source.ts        # authored content as typed TS sources + schemas.ts\\r\\n  data/*.generated.json      # generated from sources via scripts/{slug}/import-content.ts\\r\\n  modules/                   # lessons, reference, drills, practice-drills, simulator, hard-sentences, diagnostics, journal, settings, ModuleIndex\\r\\n  persistence/               # Persistence interface, LocalStorage/IndexedDB/SupabaseSynced, slug-prefixed keys\\r\\n  hooks/ lib/ styles/ types/\\r\\n  __tests__/impeccable-bans.test.ts\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Hard rules (never violate)\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Design: reuse Intro to LR exactly.** Same components, tokens, chrome, look. No per-topic themes, no bronze accents by default. A student moving between bootcamps sees one product.\\r\\n- **Pedagogy locked:** teach-first calibrate-after, never cold-test; warm-coach voice; preserve Joshua\'s exact phrasings verbatim; no em dashes anywhere.\\r\\n- **Teaching voice:** From the canonical ai-os vault root, read `wiki/teaching-voice.md`, `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`, and `wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md` before authoring or revising LSAT material. Apply the shared teaching standard to lessons, references, drills, instructions, and feedback. It governs how to teach, not the topics another bootcamp must include. Preserve Joshua\'s approved wording where he requires it; the attached voice reference calls for original examples and independently written explanations.\\r\\n- **Quality bar:** the historical `archive/wiki-pre-rebuild-2026-07-16/domains/lsat-u/synthesis/bootcamp-quality-bar.md`, `intro-to-lr-product.md`, and `intro-to-lr-design.md` references are unavailable as of 2026-10-01. Report that absence explicitly; do not invent or recreate their contents. Continue code-based inspection using the template and these explicit rules: ModuleIntro is a real lesson; mastery tracking not attempt counting; reveal panels teach; no dead clicks, no fake analytics, no raw localStorage as UI text. Final parity cannot be certified until an approved, available quality reference is established.\\r\\n- **Structure:** clone the Intro to LR module set by default. Deviations (drop/add/merge modules for topic fit) are PROPOSED at Gate 2 and require Joshua\'s approval.\\r\\n- **No invented LSAT content silently.** Content comes from Joshua\'s files. Gaps are flagged; Joshua decides (he authors, the active assistant drafts for his review, or defer to v1.5).\\r\\n- **Git:** Lovable pushes to the same origin/main. Always `git fetch` first, commit path-scoped (`git commit -- <paths>`, check `git status` for pre-staged files), never force-push. Commit, push, or deploy only when the actual task authorizes that action. A green build is not authorization. Preserve local-only review scope.\\r\\n- **Missing reviewed content counts as build failure**, not something to paper over.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Multi-session continuity (vault checkpointing)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe build spans many sessions. State lives in the Obsidian vault resolved from its `AGENTS.md`:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. On Gate 0, search `brainstorms/` for the existing topic record and reuse it for the current build checkpoint. If none exists, create a dated topic record there, never in the retired archive tree. Include a **Gate ledger** (Gate 0..5, each `pending / in progress / APPROVED YYYY-MM-DD`), module checklist, decisions, and open questions.\\r\\n2. Add a `## Now` item to `wiki/tasks.md`: `{Topic} bootcamp build (gate N)`.\\r\\n3. After EVERY gate approval and at every session end, update the build page + append one line to `wiki/log.md`.\\r\\n4. A fresh session resumes by reading the build page first, then jumping to the current gate. Never redo an APPROVED gate.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The gated process\\r\\n\\r\\n### Gate 0: Interview (no files touched yet)\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the host available question tool or ordinary conversation for unresolved scope. Reuse prior answers and approvals; do not restart approved gates. Cover:\\r\\n- Topic name, slug, route target (`/bootcamp/{slug}`)\\r\\n- What content files he will feed it, and where they are\\r\\n- Topic\'s named tools, must-preserve worked examples, signature traps (his versions of FABS / Trojan Horse)\\r\\n- Which Intro to LR modules he suspects won\'t fit this topic\\r\\n- Priority order and anything explicitly out of scope\\r\\n\\r\\nCreate the vault build page, record answers. → **Joshua approves scope.**\\r\\n\\r\\n### Gate 1: Content intake + audit\\r\\n\\r\\nSources = BOTH: (a) every file Joshua fed it and any existing authorized topic material-inventory, and (b) a scan of `corpus/Curriculum/{Topic}/` relative to the vault root (Notes/, Homework/, megadocs, scans). Follow the inventory\'s source routes and flag relevant material he did not mention. Use bounded `rg --files` and `rg` searches on this machine for gaps; report missing directories rather than assuming historical paths or Mac-only `mdfind` exist. Do not read source archives wholesale.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor every file: read it fully; summarize unique teaching content; note overlap; extract named tools, worked examples, voicy passages (mark verbatim-preserve). Tier-grade S/A/B/C/D:\\r\\n- **S** foundation, preserve verbatim where possible; **A** use heavily; **B** integrate selectively; **C** mine one block, drop rest; **D** unique-content-free, recommend drop.\\r\\n\\r\\nMap the corpus against every Intro to LR module (lessons, reference vault, indicator vault, flashcards, drills, practice drills, simulator + trap master, hard sentences, diagnostics, journal): `sufficient / marginal / insufficient` each. For each gap, ask Joshua: he authors, the active assistant drafts for review, or defer.\\r\\n\\r\\nWrite `AUDIT.md` into the vault build page folder or as a section of the build page. → **Joshua reviews the audit + gap decisions.**\\r\\n\\r\\n### Gate 2: Inspection report (before ANY code)\\r\\n\\r\\nA written plan, no code yet:\\r\\n- Proposed module set = Intro to LR clone, with any topic-fit deviations explicitly argued\\r\\n- Component reuse map: which main-conclusion components port as-is, which need topic variants, which are N/A\\r\\n- Content schema plan: the `content/*.source.ts` files to author and the import script (`scripts/{slug}/import-content.ts`) to generate `data/*.generated.json`\\r\\n- Persistence plan: slug prefix, records\\r\\n- Route wiring into the LSAT U app (mirror how main-conclusion mounts)\\r\\n- Build order (module sequence), risks, and what stays placeholder-free vs deferred\\r\\n→ **Joshua approves before a single file is written.**\\r\\n\\r\\n### Gate 3: Build, module by module\\r\\n\\r\\nScaffold the sub-app by cloning main-conclusion patterns (copy-and-adapt, do not fork the design). Then build in the approved order. Per module:\\r\\n1. Author `content/*.source.ts` from S/A-tier material (verbatim passages kept verbatim); run the import pipeline to generate data.\\r\\n2. Build the module UI from the ported components.\\r\\n3. Verify: typecheck, tests (including a `{slug}` impeccable-bans test cloned from main-conclusion), click through the surface.\\r\\n4. **Per-module sign-off from Joshua** before the next module. Commit path-scoped or push only within actual task authorization.\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 8.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"e8ac59","wall_time_seconds":0.4157074,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3920,"output":"---\\r\\ntitle: Teaching Voice\\r\\ntype: concept\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\\r\\ncreated: 2026-06-10\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nstatus: active\\r\\nsubject: Teaching Voice\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Teaching Voice\\r\\n\\r\\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools \\"preserve verbatim\\" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**LSAT teaching standard, selected 2026-10-02:** Joshua chose the supplied Conditional Reasoning transcription as the reference for how LSAT material should sound and unfold. Read [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) before drafting lessons, notes, explanations, drills, flashcards, quizzes, or instructional feedback. It contains the detailed source analysis, original calibration examples, progression, and review rubric. [Source identity and limits](sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) are preserved separately. This choice develops the teaching-content register below; correspondence and marketing retain their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Core voice (every register)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\\r\\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not \\"great job.\\"\\r\\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard (\\"expect this to be exhausting and very challenging\\"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\\r\\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: \\"partner with you on your LSAT journey,\\" \\"getting set up,\\" \\"foundational.\\" The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\\r\\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.\\r\\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype (\\"unlock,\\" \\"crush,\\" \\"level up,\\" \\"game-changer\\"; \\"crush\\" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers (\\"Sorry for such a long delay!\\"). He is not exclamation-averse.\\r\\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\\r\\n\\r\\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Greeting.** \\"Hi [Name],\\" is the default (used ~519x). \\"Hey [Name],\\" for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). \\"Hello [Name],\\" (~105x) and \\"Dear [Name],\\" (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\\r\\n- **Sign-off.** \\"Best,\\" and \\"Best regards,\\" are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). \\"Thanks,\\" when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). \\"Warmly,\\" / \\"Warm regards,\\" (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. \\"Talk soon,\\" (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\\r\\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **\\"Joshua\\"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\\r\\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** \\"let me know\\" (by far the most common, ~390x), \\"looking forward,\\" \\"I hope\\" / \\"hope all is well\\" / \\"hope you,\\" \\"no worries at all,\\" \\"happy to\\" / \\"more than happy to,\\" \\"just wanted to make sure,\\" \\"make sure,\\" \\"no rush,\\" \\"of course.\\"\\r\\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs: never.)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The apology / accountability move\\r\\n\\r\\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Hi Sandra,\\r\\n>\\r\\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Your link is the same one as before:\\r\\n> [link]\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Once again, my apologies.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Best,\\r\\n> Joshua\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** \\"I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\" The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\\r\\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** \\"To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet.\\" Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\\r\\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then \\"That is fixed now.\\"** No mechanics, no system names.\\r\\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, \\"pick any time it shows and it will book,\\" and the offer to do it by hand: \\"If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\"\\r\\n5. **Invite the next report:** \\"If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\"\\r\\n6. **Close with \\"Once again, my apologies.\\"** then \\"Best,\\" and \\"Joshua\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\\r\\n- \\"Sorry for the runaround\\" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\\r\\n- \\"You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after.\\" Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\\r\\n- \\"You have all 10 sessions\\" / \\"nothing was used from your package.\\" Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Situational micro-templates\\r\\n\\r\\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\\r\\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\\r\\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\\r\\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\\r\\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\r\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\r\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\r\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\\r\\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\\r\\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\\r\\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The three registers\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich voice governs which surface.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, personal encouragement, email\\r\\n\\r\\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\\r\\n\\r\\nReference line (target tone):\\r\\n\\r\\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.\\r\\n  > Missing this is expected. It\'s one of the hardest moves on the test. Let\'s slow down and see where it slipped.\\r\\n- **Accountability (student isn\'t doing the work):** gentle nudge, low pressure. Frame it as a slip, find a rhythm together. Never \\"the reps aren\'t happening,\\" never tough love.\\r\\n- **Praise (student nails it):** name the exact skill, tie it to their history, raise the bar.\\r\\n  > That\'s the cleanest you\'ve split premise from conclusion yet. Let\'s push it into the harder sets.\\r\\n- **Hard truth about the test:** state it plainly. \\"Do not expect for it to come easy. Expect to find this exhausting.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Teaching content (conversational reasoning) -- lessons, recaps, notes, drills, explanations\\r\\n\\r\\nUse [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md), Joshua\'s selected calibration from 2026-10-02. Keep the reader beside the teacher. Explain the ordinary meaning, ground it in an original example, make the small inferences audible, ask a useful question, and show both the consequence and its limit. Introduce terminology and notation when their meaning is established. Give each concept enough development to be learned from the page.\\r\\n\\r\\nContractions and varied sentence lengths should sound natural when read aloud. Repetition should change the reader\'s view of the same relationship. Questions should give the reader actual thinking work. Light humor may appear within an explanation when it helps understanding; this supersedes the older wit-only-at-the-edges restriction. Do not force punchlines, slang, hyperbole, catchphrases, or paragraph quotas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source governs delivery, not Joshua\'s procedure, indicator taxonomy, biography, or the scope of a lesson. Preserve his approved methods and logical precision. Personal encouragement still follows the earnest coaching register. Project-owned `lsat-u-voice` routes here and to the detailed standard; an older vendor writer-blend skill must not override this selection.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing\\r\\n\\r\\nConfident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed \\"the next card is the page in front of you\\" was clever but empty).\\r\\n\\r\\n> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.\\r\\n\\r\\nBrand copy speaks as the platform (\\"we\\", \\"you\\"), not as an individual (\\"I\\", \\"Joshua\\"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Don\'t\\r\\n\\r\\n- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)\\r\\n- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\\r\\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging (\\"you might want to\\").\\r\\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\\r\\n- No em dashes, ever.\\r\\n\\r\\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\\r\\nthe reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).\\r\\n---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text':
