(os error 3)\\nrg: src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/OpeningCourseContent.tsx: The system cannot find the path specified. (os error 3)\\nFor rule versus fact, make the hypothetical explicit: a rule tells us what would follow **if** a condition were true. It does not report that the condition happened. Contrast “Suppose this order is marked ready” with “This order is marked ready” before drawing a conclusion about an actual case. Keep the anchor sentence idiomatic and complete. Emphasis and motion around “if” should reveal that hypothetical relationship, with equally clear static information when motion is reduced.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Technical names arrive at the useful moment\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Small verbal irregularities create a person\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher\'s thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Questions that make the reader think\\r\\n\\r\\nQuestions are one of the source\'s main ways of controlling pace. They have several distinct functions. Choose the function before writing the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Kind of question | What the source does | What our version should do |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Prediction before a reveal** | Coffee/walking thought experiment asks what the reader knows, gives a blank, and says STOP. | Ask a bounded question with enough information to answer. Wait for an attempt before showing the reasoning. |\\r\\n| **One step inside a worked example** | Asks whether the sufficient condition could occur without the necessary burden. | Let the reader see the missing connection, then answer it in the walkthrough. |\\r\\n| **Meaning check** | Personalized if/then asks what the speaker knows in a proposed situation. | Read the original sentence naturally and test whether the proposed diagram preserves it. |\\r\\n| **Tempting reverse** | Tries the wrong sufficient condition and shows the interpretation failing. | Make the attractive wrong choice visible without ridiculing it. Explain the unsupported inference. |\\r\\n| **Reader objection** | FAQ voices worry about time, confidence, and whether to write. | Use the learner\'s actual objection, then explain the decision criterion. |\\r\\n| **Notation clarification** | Asks about parentheses and abbreviations. | Answer small questions where the notation first becomes relevant. |\\r\\n| **Reason for practice** | Explains why the drill asks for written contrapositives. | Say what ability the task checks and why practice may be more explicit than test-day work. |\\r\\n| **Boundary test** | Four-situation chart asks what has to happen in each case. | Compare the strongest justified answer with what could still be true. |\\r\\n\\r\\nAn exposition question can receive an immediate answer. A student question intended to train reasoning needs an actual pause. Do not ask “What do you think?” and reveal the answer in the same breath. Do not add a rhetorical question to every paragraph merely to sound interactive.\\r\\n\\r\\nA useful check has three parts: a precise prompt, a learner decision, and a reasoned explanation. For example:\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Rule:** Every blue-sticker parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> **Fact:** This parcel has been inspected.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Does the parcel have to have a blue sticker? Explain what the rule says about that direction.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback should identify the gap: the rule covers blue-sticker parcels; it hasn\'t said all inspected parcels have a blue sticker. “Incorrect. Review sufficient and necessary” is too thin.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Transitions explain why the next move follows\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source\'s transitions keep the learner oriented. “So far” recovers what is established. “Let\'s return” reuses an anchor. “Now that we have a handle on” names the prerequisite for notation. “This leads me to the next question” connects a practical worry to its answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nA transition should answer one of these:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. What have we established that the next idea uses?\\r\\n2. What is still unclear?\\r\\n3. What will we change while holding the rule fixed?\\r\\n4. Why is the next representation useful now?\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal transitions:\\r\\n\\r\\n> We know what the sticker tells us. Now let\'s see what the inspection stamp tells us.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> These sentences use different words, but they describe the same relationship. Let\'s write that relationship once.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> The arrow is pointing the right way. Check one more thing: did we keep the whole condition?\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not fill the gap with a curriculum announcement such as “Next, we will explore necessary conditions.” Say why the requirement is the next thing worth examining.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Repetition changes the reader\'s view\\r\\n\\r\\nThe breathing/air anchor appears in the opening, indicator families, contrapositive, fact chart, diagram, and target analysis. The reader keeps the same relationship while the representation changes. That saves the effort of understanding a new scenario at every turn.\\r\\n\\r\\nWithin the contrapositive explanation, the source repeats the requirement in several forms: air is needed; breathing has to have air; without air the requirement is missing; the original condition cannot hold. Each repetition supplies a step or a new angle.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor our writing, reuse an anchor while doing different work:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read the ordinary meaning.\\r\\n2. Apply it to a case.\\r\\n3. Identify the sufficient and necessary ideas.\\r\\n4. Compare an unsupported reverse.\\r\\n5. Reword the same relationship.\\r\\n# Copy corrections\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery student-facing wording fix, with the reason it was wrong. Read this before\\r\\nwriting or editing copy for LSAT U so the same mistake is not made twice.\\r\\n\\r\\nCompanion to [[teaching-voice]], which says how the writing should sound. This\\r\\nsays what has actually gone wrong and why.\\r\\n\\r\\nStarted 2026-09-07 from a Read, Recall, Colour pass. Add to it every time a\\r\\ncorrection is made.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The rules these corrections came from\\r\\n\\r\\nThree rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was\\r\\nenforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface\\r\\nadded later starts uncovered.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.\\r\\n- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`\\r\\n  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.\\r\\n- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nGuards now live with the content rather than with each component:\\r\\n`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every\\r\\nsurface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Corrections\\r\\n\\r\\n### Conditional opening: define the words before using them (2026-10-03)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing what that meant. Teach what a rule lets you conclude in plain language, then attach the vocabulary to a concrete relationship. Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required before relying on those roles. Show what the must-arrow records and why English clause order does not determine the two sides.\\r\\n\\r\\n2026-10-03 clarification: explicitly distinguish the indicator from the complete condition it refers to. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. The indicator itself is not a condition in the diagram. Show the same relationship with if at the beginning and in the middle, and show necessary targets both before and after requirement language. Neither sentence position nor the words immediately following the indicator determine placement. Preserve if versus only if as different complete expressions.\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. Replace compressed ready-order wording with a natural full sentence. Give each paragraph and interaction a specific teaching job. These corrections supplement the canonical voice standard rather than adding a new writing register.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: \\"Read each whole idea,\\" \\"Find what the statements guarantee,\\" the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact and rule definitions must explain the inference (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected definitions that remained abstract and an explanation that did not clearly distinguish applying a rule from linking rules. Define the inputs with examples, then show the matching step and exact output. A case fact plus a rule proves something about that case. Two connecting rules produce another conditional rule without establishing a case exists. Adding a fact to the chain is a separate step. Explain all four fact situations and a hidden chain revealed by contraposition rather than leaving them implicit in an interactive model. Rules include general logical relationships, not only policies or obligations.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Boxed `L -> R` → bold teaching notation **L → R** (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected the recap\'s small monospace arrow inside a pale code box. It looked like implementation text and weakened the explanation. Use a real, clearly weighted arrow in ordinary teaching typography. Explain its complete conditions before shortening them. The Some, Most, and All reference also governs the progression: introduce the whole idea, develop the individual components, then compare and combine them. A visual refresh must review that sequence, not only colors and boxes.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Concept lesson\\" → the actual reasoning title (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe recap preview exposed an internal label above the student lesson. Joshua explicitly rejected it. Use a title such as \\"Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\" and headings that teach the reasoning. Keep production labels, version metadata, and design commentary in private records. Check the rendered HTML, since its shell can introduce wording absent from the Markdown source.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Recall Rephrase Colour\\" → \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\"\\r\\nThe door\'s heading was hardcoded to a name the product does not have, one line\\r\\nunder a chip reading \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\". \\"Rephrase\\" belongs to a different\\r\\n"}'}]

Page renamed **Find the complete condition**. Replaced seven-expression selector, repeated definitions/arrow lecture and embedded flashcards with three sequential sections:
1. Registered guests: preserve the restriction.
2. Report submitted before noon: retain deadline despite reordered clause, then late-applicant check.
3. Read expressions in context: indirect-question caution, “the only” explanation, notebook check.

Existing examples reused from GroupLessons and reviewed Group 1 plan. Checks adapted existing applicant/notebook questions, adding incomplete-condition distractors. All choices provide specific feedback after selection.

Purpose now distinct: learn to select the **full sufficient condition** before sufficient-practice. Later “Keep the whole meaning” remains broader cross-group synthesis, including necessary targets and multiple translation checks.

- Focused tests: **14 passed**, rerun after test API correction.
- Typecheck: **passed**.
- Parent completed browser checks and build.
- No commit/deploy.

The required result is present.</td></tr>\\r\\n        <tr><td>No</td><td>Yes</td><td>Yes. The rule allows a bicycle without a win.</td></tr>\\r\\n        <tr><td>No</td><td>No</td><td>Yes. With no win, the rule settles nothing about a bicycle.</td></tr>\\r\\n        <tr><td>Yes</td><td>No</td><td><strong>No. This is the single violation.</strong></td></tr>\\r\\n      </tbody>\\r\\n    </table>\\r\\n    <p>A conditional is broken when its sufficient condition is present and its necessary condition is absent. That\'s why no bicycle rules out a win: a winner without a bicycle would be the forbidden combination.</p>\\r\\n  </NoteSection>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction groupPracticeTasks(group: \'group-1\' | \'group-2\') {\\r\\n  const content = sections(GroupPractice({ group }).props.children);\\r\\n  const recall = content[content.length - 1];\\r\\n  return content.slice(0, -1).map((item, index, items) => ({\\r\\n    title: item.props.title ?? \'Read the whole rule\',\\r\\n    content: <>{item}{index === items.length - 1 && recall}</>,\\r\\n  }));\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction SufficientPractice() {\\r\\n  return <PracticeTaskRunner rounds={[{ title: \'Build the sufficient side\', tasks: [\\r\\n    ...groupPracticeTasks(\'group-1\'),\\r\\n    { title: \'Build a case that proves the limit\', content: <NoteSection title=\\"Build a case that proves the limit\\"><CounterexampleLab/></NoteSection> },\\r\\n  ] }]}/>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction ContrapositivePractice() {\\r\\n  const [current, setCurrent] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [checked, setChecked] = useState<boolean[]>(() => referencePracticeItems.map(() => false));\\r\\n  return <NoteSection title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n    <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking. If an answer needs revision, inspect the direction, qualifications and negation, then retry.</p>\\r\\n    <p className=\\"co-kicker\\" role=\\"status\\">Sentence {current + 1} of {referencePracticeItems.length}</p>\\r\\n    {referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <div key={item.sentence} hidden={current !== index} data-course-practice-item={index + 1}><IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1} onChecked={value => setChecked(previous => previous.map((old, itemIndex) => itemIndex === index ? value : old))}/></div>)}\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button type=\\"button\\" disabled={current === 0} onClick={() => setCurrent(current - 1)}>Previous sentence</button><button type=\\"button\\" disabled={current === referencePracticeItems.length - 1 || !checked[current]} onClick={() => setCurrent(current + 1)}>Next sentence</button></div>\\r\\n    <p>Moving between sentences keeps your choices and feedback.</p>\\r\\n  </NoteSection>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction Content({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  switch (id) {\\r\\n    case \'meaning\': return <ReferenceBody number={1} omitTransition/>;\\r\\n    case \'sufficient\': return <ReferenceBody number={2} omitTransition/>;\\r\\n    case \'necessary\': return <ReferenceBody number={4} omitTransition/>;\\r\\n    case \'must-arrow\': return <MustArrowNote/>;\\r\\n    case \'sufficient-words\': return <CompleteConditionNote/>;\\r\\n    case \'necessary-words\': return <><IndicatorNote group=\\"group-2\\"/><PermissionBoundary/></>;\\r\\n    case \'whole-meaning\': return <WholeMeaningNote/>;\\r\\n    case \'missing-requirement\': return <><ReferenceBody number={6}/><ReferenceBody number={7} omitTransition/></>;\\r\\n    case \'contrapositive\': return <><ReferenceBody number={9}/><details className=\\"co-practical-note\\"><summary>Practical questions about diagramming</summary><ReferenceBody number={10} omitTransition/></details><NoteSection title=\\"Two possible rooms, one limit\\"><NegativeCaseLab/></NoteSection><CompatibleCasesRecap/></>;\\r\\n    case \'foundation-practice\': return <FoundationPractice/>;\\r\\n    case \'sufficient-practice\': return <SufficientPractice/>;\\r\\n    case \'mixed-practice\': return <OpeningMixedDrill/>;\\r\\n    case \'contrapositive-practice\': return <ContrapositivePractice/>;\\r\\n    default: throw new Error(`Unknown opening course content: ${id}`);\\r\\n  }\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n/** Article body only. Course routes own titles, navigation and completion. */\\r\\nexport function OpeningCourseContent({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  return <div className={id === \'foundation-practice\' ? \'opening-course-content\' : \'co-opening cr-sequence opening-course-content\'}><Content key={id} id={id}/></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]

Question-specific purpose and explanation quality need review before treating every filter match as a useful conditional lesson. |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Assignment and self-study boundaries\\r\\n\\r\\nThe newer local bootcamp registry **does register Conditional Indicators Refresher** at `/conditional-indicators-refresher`, so the whole refresher can be assigned through the bootcamp mechanism. Leaving out wearing a gold badge would make a claim about all guests.\', instruction: \'Choose the description that keeps the restriction in each sentence.\' },\\r\\n  { title: \'Build the diagram\', purpose: \'If the condition on the left is true, what must follow on the right?\', example: \'If a parcel has a blue sticker, it has been inspected.\', explanation: \'A blue sticker is enough to establish inspection. Read the diagram in English before checking.\' },\\r\\n  { title: \'Translate the sentence yourself\', purpose: \'Write your own diagram before looking at supplied conditions.\', example: \'Only members use the studio.\', explanation: \'First decide what the sentence says you need in order to use the studio. Compare with the choices only after making that attempt.\' },\\r\\n  { title: \'Does the meaning change?\', purpose: \'Different wording can preserve a rule or change what it promises.\', example: \'Every parcel in Locker 4 has been scanned.\', explanation: \'This tells you something about parcels in Locker 4. It leaves scanned parcels elsewhere possible. Use that distinction when comparing another way of wording a rule.\', instruction: \'Compare what each sentence requires. []))];\\r\\n  return errors.flatMap(error => {\\r\\n    const items = error === \'equivalence\' && results[\'mixed-transfer-4\']?.errors.includes(\'equivalence\') ? [obligationConfirmationItem] : confirmationItemsByError[error].slice(0, 1);\\r\\n    return items.map(item => ({ id: item.id, stage: 5, kind: \'choice\' as const, item }));\\r\\n  });\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction ChoiceQuestion({ item, onCheck }: { item: MixedDrillChoiceItem; onCheck: Check }) {\\r\\n  const [picked, setPicked] = useState<number | null>(null);\\r\\n  const firstAnswer = useRef<HTMLButtonElement>(null);\\r\\n  const [checked, setChecked] = useState(false);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"omd-question\\">\\r\\n    <blockquote>{item.sentence}</blockquote><h3>{item.prompt}</h3>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"omd-options\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Answer choices\\">{item.options.map((option, index) => <button ref={index === 0 ? firstAnswer : undefined} type=\\"button\\" key={option} aria-pressed={picked === index} onClick={() => { setPicked(index); setChecked(false); onCheck(null); }}>{option}</button>)}</div>\\r\\n    <button type=\\"button\\" className=\\"omd-primary\\" disabled={picked === null} onClick={() => { setChecked(true); onCheck(picked === item.correct, picked === item.correct ? [] : [item.errorByOption?.[picked] ?? item.skill]); }}>Check answer</button>\\r\\n    <div role=\\"status\\" aria-atomic=\\"true\\" className={checked ? \'omd-feedback\' : undefined} data-correct={checked ? picked === item.correct : undefined}>{checked && <><p>{item.feedback[picked]}</p>\\r\\n      {picked !== item.correct && <button type=\\"button\\" onClick={() => { setChecked(false); setPicked(null); onCheck(null); firstAnswer.current?.focus(); }}>Try this question again</button>}\\r\\n    </>}</div>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction DrillQuestion({ question, onCheck }: { question: Question; onCheck: Check }) {\\r\\n  if (question.kind === \'choice\') return <ChoiceQuestion item={question.item} onCheck={onCheck}/>;\\r\\n  if (question.kind === \'independent\') return <MixedItemCheck item={question.item} index={question.itemIndex} embedded onChecked={() => undefined} onResult={result => {\\r\\n    if (!result) return onCheck(null);\\r\\n    const errors: MixedDrillError[] = [];\\r\\n    if (!result.targetCorrect) errors.push(result.targetIncomplete ? \'qualification\' : \'direction\');\\r\\n    if (!result.sideCorrect) errors.push(question.item.family === \'necessary\' ? \'requirement\' : \'direction\');\\r\\n    onCheck(result.targetCorrect && result.sideCorrect, errors);\\r\\n  }}/>;\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"cir-root omd-diagram\\"><p>Build the original diagram, then read it as an if/then sentence before checking.</p>\\r\\n    <DiagramDrills items={[question.item]} embedded persistProgress={false} allowContrapositive={false} allowNegation={false} shuffleItems={false} explanations={openingActivityExplanations} feedbackForResult={result => {\\r\\n      if (result.correct) return `Your diagram keeps ${question.item.left} as sufficient for ${question.item.right}.`;\\r\\n      const reversed = result.left === question.item.right && result.right === question.item.left;\\r\\n      if (reversed) return `Your arrow makes ${question.item.right} enough to establish ${question.item.left}. Use each condition once: ${question.item.left} on the left, ${question.item.right} on the right. Extra or repeated words change the diagram.`;\\r\\n    }} onResult={result => {\\r\\n      if (!result) return onCheck(null);\\r\\n      const reversed = result.left === question.item.right && result.right === question.item.left;\\r\\n      onCheck(result.correct, result.correct ? [] : [reversed && question.item.group === \'g2\' ? \'requirement\' : \'direction\']);\\r\\n    }}/>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst RetainedQuestion = memo(function RetainedQuestion({ question, record }: {\\r\\n  question: Question;\\r\\n  record: (id: string, correct: boolean | null, errors?: MixedDrillError[]) => void;\\r\\n}) {\\r\\n  return <DrillQuestion question={question} onCheck={(correct, errors) => record(question.id, correct, errors)}/>;\\r\\n});\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function OpeningMixedDrill() {\\r\\n  const [questions, setQuestions] = useState<Question[]>(baselineQuestions);\\r\\n  const [current, setCurrent] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [visited, setVisited] = useState([baselineQuestions[0].id]);\\r\\n  const [results, setResults] = useState<Record<string, Result>>({});\\r\\n  const [finished, setFinished] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const [introducedStages, setIntroducedStages] = useState<number[]>([]);\\r\\n  const heading = useRef<HTMLHeadingElement>(null);\\r\\n  const runner = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);\\r\\n  const question = questions[current];\\r\\n  const stage = stages[question.stage];\\r\\n  const showIntroduction = !finished && !introducedStages.includes(question.stage);\\r\\n"}'}]

This note summarizes existing teaching evidence and supplies retrieval pointers; it does not reproduce third-party transcripts or drills.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Start LSAT Lab discovery at its topic library\\r\\n\\r\\nThe saved 2026-09-27 platform audit records [Video Library](https://app.lsatlab.com/video-library/) with **Lessons grouped by topic**, plus **Recordings** searchable/filterable by section, format, difficulty and teacher. Full caption archive, private adapted teacher script, Socratic cues, boards and five worked questions. | Core opening explicitly separates three operations: contrapose; connect rules; use a fact to trigger a rule. Explains why a shared result is not enough to connect rules, certainty versus mere frequency, illegal reversal/negation, useful rather than automatic diagramming, and intermediate chain inferences. Applications include requirements, AND/OR, quantifier/subject scope, passive voice and answer strength. |\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful source intervals for comparison, from the existing research summary:\\r\\n\\r\\n- 00:02:22–00:11:14: three conditional skills.\\r\\n- 00:03:03–00:07:47: valid bridges versus shared-result non-links; intermediate chain conclusions.\\r\\n- 00:08:18–00:10:07: contraposition exposes a link or fact-triggering inference.\\r\\n- 00:11:15–00:12:45: invalid moves and fluency through repetition.\\r\\n- 00:13:00–00:15:18: certainty identifies a standard conditional; a frequency qualifier can defeat an apparent indicator.\\r\\n- 01:21:45–01:23:07: when diagramming helps.\\r\\n- 01:25:52–01:27:59: test a conditional answer from its trigger and usable positions.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are summaries of one lesson, not LSAT Lab\'s full curriculum. No separate archived Conditional Logic, Quantity, Complex Linking or Missing Link lecture ID was found in this routed review.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Official public sources already identified locally\\r\\n\\r\\nThe saved report `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/research/2026-09-12-lsat-lab-facts-rules-and-teaching-note-writing-report.md` names:\\r\\n\\r\\n- [LSAT Lab Conditional Logic](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jeDnykOrswY).\\r\\n- [LSAT Lab Must Be True](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uywHnsMelJg).\\r\\n- [Updated LR cram sheet](https://assets.lsatlab.com/uploads/2024/07/2024-Updated-LR-cram-sheet.pdf).\\r\\n- [Standards-committee explanation, PT149 S1 Q18](https://www.lsatlab.com/lsat-explanations/PT149-S1-Q18/if-the-standards-committee-has-a-quorum), used locally to illustrate that rules can support a rule inference without supplying facts.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are source pointers from prior research, not freshly accessed sources. One breaks the rule.\\">\\r\\n    <blockquote>If you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.</blockquote>\\r\\n    <p>The rule requires a bicycle when you win. Let\'s compare all four possible combinations. These are possible cases, not a claim that any of them happened.</p>\\r\\n    <table className=\\"co-combination-table\\">\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction TranslationLab({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [choice, setChoice] = useState<string | null>(null);\\r\\n  const [diagram, setDiagram] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const item = translationExamples[id][index];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-lab\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Which words does the expression refer to?</p><div className=\\"co-word-tabs\\" role=\\"group\\" aria-label=\\"Indicator examples\\">{translationExamples[id].map((e, i) => <button key={e.word} aria-pressed={i === index} onClick={() => { setIndex(i); setChoice(null); setDiagram(false); }}>{e.word}</button>)}</div><TargetSentence item={item} marked={choice === item.target}/><p>What is <strong>{item.word}</strong> referring to?</p><div className=\\"co-choices\\">{orderedConditionalIdeas(item).map(value => <button key={value} aria-pressed={choice === value} onClick={() => { setChoice(value); setDiagram(false); }}>{value}</button>)}</div><div role=\\"status\\">{choice && <div className={`co-feedback ${choice === item.target ? \'co-correct\' : \'\'}`}><span className=\\"co-answer-state\\">{choice === item.target ? \'Correct target.\' : \'Not quite.\'}</span><strong>{choice === item.target ? \'You have the complete target.\' : \'You chose the other condition.\'}</strong>{choice !== item.target && <p>You chose {choice}, but {item.word} refers to {item.target}. {id === \'group-1\' ? \'You selected the result rather than the complete sufficient idea.\' : \'You selected the condition that needs the requirement rather than the required idea.\'}</p>}<p>{item.why}</p>{choice === item.target && <button className=\\"co-primary\\" onClick={() => setDiagram(true)}>Show the {id === \'group-1\' ? \'sufficient condition on the left\' : \'requirement on the right\'}</button>}</div>}</div>{diagram && <Arrow left={item.left} right={item.right}/>}</div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction Flashcards({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  const cards = flashcardExamples[id];\\r\\n  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [back, setBack] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const faceId = useId();\\r\\n  const card = cards[index];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-flash\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Recall before revealing · {index + 1} / {cards.length}</p>\\r\\n    <div className={`co-flash-face ${back ? \'co-flipped\' : \'\'}`}><strong>{card.sentence}</strong><span>Say the complete rule, then compare.</span><div id={faceId} aria-live=\\"polite\\">{back && <><strong>{card.readback}</strong><p>{card.why}</p></>}</div></div>\\r\\n    <div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button aria-expanded={back} aria-controls={faceId} onClick={() => setBack(!back)}>{back ? \'Hide answer\' : \'Reveal answer\'}</button><button disabled={index === 0} onClick={() => { setIndex(index - 1); setBack(false); }}>Previous sentence</button><button onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % cards.length); setBack(false); }}>{index === cards.length - 1 ? \'Start again\' : \'Next sentence\'}</button></div><p className=\\"co-small\\">These examples help you recall the relationship. Your attached drill gives you separate questions.</p></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction DirectionSwitch() {\\r\\n  const [only, setOnly] = useState(false);\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-switch\\"><div className=\\"co-choices\\"><button aria-pressed={!only} onClick={() => setOnly(false)}>if</button><button aria-pressed={only} onClick={() => setOnly(true)}>only if</button></div><blockquote>A student enters <mark>{only ? \'only if\' : \'if\'}</mark> the student has a pass.</blockquote><Arrow left={only ? \'Enters\' : \'Has pass\'} right={only ? \'Has pass\' : \'Enters\'}/><p role=\\"status\\">{only ? \'The pass is required. Having one does not establish entry.\' : \'This sentence guarantees entry for a student who has a pass.\'}</p></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\nfunction DiagramBuilder({ id }: { id: string }) {\\r\\n  const items = diagramExamples[id];\\r\\n  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);\\r\\n  const [left, setLeft] = useState<string | null>(null);\\r\\n  const [checked, setChecked] = useState(false);\\r\\n  const item = items[index];\\r\\n  return <div className=\\"co-lab\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Build the diagram</p><TargetSentence item={item} marked={checked && left === item.left}/><p>Both complete ideas are below. Choose the sufficient condition, the one that gives you enough to establish the other. It belongs on the left of the arrow.</p><div className=\\"co-choices\\">{orderedConditionalIdeas(item).map(value => <button key={value} aria-pressed={left === value} onClick={() => { setLeft(value); setChecked(false); }}>{value}</button>)}</div>{left && <><Arrow left={left} right={left === item.target ? item.other : item.target}/><button className=\\"co-primary\\" onClick={() => setChecked(true)}>Check my diagram</button></>}<div role=\\"status\\">{checked && <div className={`co-feedback ${left === item.left ? \'co-correct\' : \'\'}`}><strong>{left === item.left ? \'Your arrow preserves the relationship.\' : \'Your arrow reverses this relationship.\'}</strong>{left !== item.left && <p>{item.reversedPromise}</p>}<p>{item.why}</p><p>{item.readback}</p><button onClick={() => { setIndex((index + 1) % items.length); setLeft(null); setChecked(false); }}>{index === items.length - 1 ? \'Practice these again\' : \'Next sentence\'}</button></div>}</div></div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\ntype IndicatorExplanation = {\\r\\n  title: string;\\r\\n  paragraphs: string[];\\r\\n  prompt: string;\\r\\n  reasoning: string[];\\r\\n  after?: string[];\\r\\n  check?: { prompt: string; options: string[]; correct: number; feedback: string[] };\\r\\n};\\r\\n\\r\\nconst indicatorExplanations: Record<string, IndicatorExplanation[]> = {\\r\\n  \'group-1\': [\\r\\n    {\\r\\n      title: \'If: the starting condition can come last\',\\r\\n      paragraphs: [\\r\\n        \\"Read the if-clause first: the report is submitted before noon. The sentence makes that enough for review. Every report meeting that condition is reviewed.\\",\\r\\n        \\"The target of if is the complete idea submitted before noon. It appears second in the sentence, but it still has the sufficient role. Moving a clause doesn\'t change what guarantees what.\\",\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n      prompt: \'Which fact lets you establish review: submission before noon, or review itself?\',\\r\\n      reasoning: [\\r\\n        \\"Submission before noon. That\'s the condition the rule makes enough for review. Start there and the sentence gives you the other condition.\\",\\r\\n        \\"Starting from review instead would make a new promise about submission time. The original sentence hasn\'t told us that every reviewed report arrived before noon.\\",\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n      after: [\\r\\n        \\"If can do another job. In The archivist asked if the drawer was locked, the sentence reports a question. You can read asked if as asked whether. It doesn\'t say a locked drawer guarantees an inquiry, or that an inquiry proves the drawer is locked.\\",\\r\\n"}'}]

Statement 7 The sun will rise if you pay your taxes. Statement 8 Whenever it rains it pours. Statement 9 All the Kings men swore an oath of allegiance. Statement 26 Where the judges are independent there will be a good legal system. Statement 27 All trees are perennial plants with an elongated stem. Statement 28 Cyrenaic hedonism believes pleasure is the only good. Statement 29 Every mating ritual can be conceptualized as a game to illuminate the underlying strategies. Statement 34 Economic conditions improved only when the policy of free trade had been implemented. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 8.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"793209","wall_time_seconds":0.6052592,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":286,"output":"corpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:15:  - {\\"path\\": \\"IF_THEN worksheet.pdf\\", \\"modified\\": \\"2025-10-31T10:21:47.844000+00:00\\", \\"word_count\\": 497, \\"doc_id\\": null}\\r\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:17:  - {\\"path\\": \\"LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Necessary/Notes/intro training group \\\\u00bd (3).pdf\\", \\"modified\\": \\"2025-05-01T01:05:34+00:00\\", \\"word_count\\": 470, \\"doc_id\\": null}\\r\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:90:## Source: IF_THEN worksheet.pdf\\r\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:92:**Path:** IF_THEN worksheet.pdf  **Modified:** 2025-10-31T10:21:47.844000+00:00  **Words:** 497  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:106:## Source: intro training group ½ (3).pdf\\r\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:108:**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Necessary/Notes/intro training group ½ (3).pdf  **Modified:** 2025-05-01T01:05:34+00:00  **Words:** 470  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"1de319","wall_time_seconds":0.3132144,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2068,"output":"\\r\\nThese are authoring sections, not difficulty levels, assignment labels, or a student-facing catalogue. Each can contain several short notes and interactions. Joshua assigns the appropriate lesson or practice; a student sees that assigned work.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Order | What we teach, in sequence | What the learner does here |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **1. What a conditional tells you** | Define a condition as an idea that can be true or false. Define a conditional through if/then. Explain what the relationship lets us know and why that matters for LSAT conclusions. Read one original rule naturally. Explain that must rules out an exception to the stated relationship. Distinguish imagining the if-condition from reporting that it occurred. | Read the rule; describe what would follow **if** its first condition were true. A brief meaning check comes before notation. |\\r\\n| **2. Sufficient: enough to establish the other condition** | Name the if-part. Explain that it may or may not be true. If it is absent, this rule alone does not settle the other condition. If it is present, the required condition follows. Define using or activating the rule through that present sufficient condition. | Compare the same rule with a sufficient-present fact and a sufficient-absent fact. Explain both conclusions before revealing the answers. |\\r\\n| **3. The sufficient word family** | Introduce the family in the source\'s row order: if; when/whenever; any/anytime; all; every/every time; in order to; people who; each. Show equivalent ways to express one relationship. Explain grouped forms and optional endings. Explain why understanding the sentence matters more than spotting a word. | Compare ordinary-English versions of a stable relationship; identify the complete sufficient idea. No arrow-construction task yet. Add current Group 1 expressions as clearly contextual extensions here. |\\r\\n| **4. Necessary: what has to be true** | Name the then-part. Explain its required role when the sufficient condition is true. Establish certainty about that requirement. Avoid implying that the condition must happen later in time. | In the same anchor, explain what is required and why. Keep the sufficient and necessary roles visible as complete ideas. |\\r\\n| **5. The necessary word family** | Follow the source\'s row order: then; must; necessary; required; only/only if; depends; need/need to; have to; essential; precondition. Show how these express the same relationship as the earlier family. Give only if special attention: it identifies a requirement despite containing if. Explain that symbols will help keep the relationship clear when wording changes. | Read and compare necessary-language sentences, including a controlled if versus only-if contrast. Add current Group 2 forms here without silently reclassifying Joshua\'s groups. |\\r\\n| **6. Discover the contrapositive** | Restate the sufficient-to-necessary relationship. Use a fresh thought experiment. Remove the necessary condition and ask whether the sufficient condition could still be true. Pause. Derive the answer from the unmet requirement. Name the contrapositive only after that reasoning, then return to the anchor. | Predict before reveal, explain the contradiction in ordinary English, and apply the reasoning to the anchor. Do not start with flip-and-negate. |\\r\\n| **7. Compare all four situations** | Teach in the source\'s chart order: sufficient present; necessary absent; sufficient absent; necessary present. The first establishes the necessary condition; the second rules out the sufficient condition; the other two leave the other condition unsettled by this rule alone. Explain the tempting reverse and inverse at this point. | Use a four-case interaction. State the strongest justified conclusion, then show an allowed case where an unsupported guess fails. Keep the rule available for later facts. |\\r\\n| **8. Write the relationship with an arrow** | Recover both word families. Introduce sufficient on the left, necessary on the right and the must-arrow between them. Explain implication, not chronology or causation. Build a first diagram in the source\'s construction order: notice the indicator; draw the arrow; abbreviate the complete target on its role side; fill the other condition. Explain each abbreviation. | Watch a narrated construction, then place complete ideas and read the result back in English. Sentence order can differ from diagram order. |\\r\\n| **9. Write and read the contrapositive** | Diagram the meaning already learned. Introduce the not-sign. Work through all four sign patterns: neither side negative, necessary negative, sufficient negative, both negative. Derive reverse-and-negate as a shortcut. Explain equivalence. Then distinguish writing the contrapositive from reading it when useful. | Build and read each form; explain why the transformed rule says the same thing. Teach Joshua\'s full five-step routine here: indicator; two complete ideas; role placement; contrapositive; English readback. |\\r\\n| **10. Answer practical diagramming questions** | Keep the source\'s FAQ order: when to diagram; how to check correctness and build confidence; concern about time; when to write a contrapositive; why training still asks for written contrapositives; how to choose readable abbreviations. | Compare a diagram with the original sentence. Diagnose a specific mistranslation and record what caused it. Start untimed; timing can be added to teacher-assigned medium/advanced practice. |\\r\\n| **11. Find the indicator\'s target** | Define target. Mark complete targets in familiar sentences. First show the usual position after an indicator. Then show targets before an indicator. Then show the same indicator moving relative to its target. The source\'s obscured line remains missing evidence, not a sentence to reconstruct. | Highlight the complete idea, including qualifications; compare meaning-preserving word-order variants. The closest noun or next word is not an automatic target rule. |\\r\\n| **12. Check with Personalized If/Then** | Define the translation check and why it helps. Read ordinary meaning; propose the sufficient condition; imagine a case meeting it; ask what must be true. Work a correct interpretation, then a deliberately reversed interpretation and explain why the latter fails. Finish by recommending the meaning check. | Perform both readings against the same whole sentence. Keep who or what the sentence concerns intact. Extend with Joshua\'s What Test as a complementary meaning check. |\\r\\n| **13. Practice the whole process** | Explain the drill\'s purpose, permit looking back, and require an original diagram and contrapositive. Begin with straightforward sufficient language, then varied sentence placement and necessary language. The source\'s six items model a basic translation set, not complete family coverage. | Attempt before answers. Use fresh original items and explain the target, roles, original, contrapositive and English readback. Provide both targeted family practice and a mixed transfer set. |\\r\\n\\r\\n### An original anchor for the rebuild\\r\\n\\r\\n**If an order is marked ready for pickup, it has been packed.**\\r\\n\\r\\n- Opening: imagine an order with the ready label. Packing follows under the stated rule; the sentence hasn\'t said an actual order has the label.\\r\\n- Sufficient: a ready label is enough to establish packing. Without the label, packing remains possible.\\r\\n- Necessary: packing is required for an order carrying that label.\\r\\n- Contrapositive: an order that hasn\'t been packed cannot be marked ready under this rule. Let the student work this out before naming it.\\r\\n- Four situations: ready implies packed; not packed implies not ready; not ready leaves packing open; packed leaves the ready label open.\\r\\n- Notation, introduced later: Ready → Packed; Not packed → Not ready. Abbreviate only after defining the complete labels.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis anchor is our own course example. Reuse it when a new view deepens the idea, and use fresh situations for transfer. A statement such as a published report requiring editor approval can provide a separate contrapositive prediction: no editor approval means it cannot be published under the rule. Do not treat an obligation or permission as proof that someone complied or acted.\\r\\n\\r\\n### How the current material fits\\r\\n"}'}]

## assistant

Read-only mapping complete. **The app has most of the teaching operations, but the restored Desktop worksheets can recover sustained practice and answer-key evidence missing from legacy excerpts.**

| Current destination | Actual learner work | Skeleton connection |
|---|---|---|
| Sufficient / Necessary | Understand the relationship, compare cases, see Group 1/2 language | Meaning and word families |
| Must arrow | Place complete conditions left/right; read the arrow; avoid reversal | Write/read implication |
| Drill 1 | 24 checks across definitions, roles, sides, indicators and fact application | Foundation consolidation |
| Find the complete condition | Keep modifiers; locate a later if-clause; parse “the only”; two selection checks | Indicator target / complete sufficient condition |
| Build the sufficient side | Choose target from supplied phrases; build diagram from supplied conditions; “the only” and equivalence checks; construct counterexample | Guided Group 1 translation |
| Necessary-condition words | Seven expression panels, requirement contrasts, permission/instruction boundary, embedded recall | Group 2 contextual reading |
| Keep the whole meaning | Target position, qualifications, only/the-only, Personalized If/Then | Cross-group translation checks |
| Conditional translation practice | Complete-condition selection; diagram construction; written attempt before choices; equivalence; unfamiliar wording; error-specific fresh confirmation | Mixed transfer |
| Missing requirement / Contrapositive / final drill | Four fact cases, missing necessary condition, original and contrapositive diagrams/readback | Remaining skeleton stages |

**Concrete gaps and overlap**

Now we add information about Maya and examine what that statement lets us conclude.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-sufficiency\\"><SufficientConditionFilm/></div>\\r\\n      <p>Starting from getting a bicycle and concluding that you win is the tempting <strong>reverse</strong>. While the provided sources do not detail every single LSAT Logical Reasoning question type (such as Parallel Reasoning or Method of Reasoning) they offer a comprehensive breakdown of the core activities required for analysis and specifically target the most critical error tested in Flaw questions. The breakdown of conditional logic primarily revolves around two main analytical activities: Chaining (deriving new rules) and Triggering (deriving new facts). I. General Breakdown Activity: Chaining Conditionals (Deriving New Rules). Chaining involves synthesizing multiple conditional rules to create a new inferred conditional statement. This is crucial for questions that require you to draw broad conclusions from a set of premises (often seen in Inference/Must Be True contexts). Connecting Rules: You must determine if the necessary condition (right side) of one conditional statement matches the sufficient condition (left side) of another. For instance if A rightarrow B and B rightarrow C they chain to infer a new conditional relationship: A rightarrow C. Contraposing Chains: You can contraposit an entire chain starting from the final necessary condition and negating back to the initial sufficient condition. If the chain is A rightarrow D the contrapositive is If not D then not A. The LSAT commonly rewards the ability to connect the beginning of the chain to the ending of the chain. Negating Complex Ideas: If the idea being contraposed (the trigger or the outcome) is a complex idea connected by and or or (e.g. A and B) you must remember to flip the truth values when negating. And turns into or and or turns into and. This ensures you correctly flip the truth value of everything in the complex idea. II. General Breakdown Activity: Triggering Conditionals (Deriving New Facts). Triggering is the process of using a known fact to activate a conditional rule and derive a certain factual inference. This is the process utilized in many Must Be True and Inference questions. A conditional rule (Sufficient rightarrow Necessary) can only be used in two valid scenarios: 1. Affirming the Sufficient Condition: If a fact confirms that the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) is true then the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) must necessarily follow. 2. Denying the Necessary Condition (Contrapositive Logic): If a fact confirms that the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) is not true then the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) cannot have been true. If the fact relates to the conditional rule in any other way (e.g. affirming the necessary or denying the sufficient) the rule just disappears and has no power over us. III. Breakdown in Specific Question Types and Contexts. Flaw Questions. Flaw questions are specifically designed to test whether you can recognize errors in conditional reasoning. The Conditional Flaw: The specific mistake frequently made by LSAT authors is confusing the necessary and the sufficient. This happens when an argument presents a rule (A rightarrow B) observes that the right side (the necessary condition B) is happening and then attempts to conclude that this proves the left side (the sufficient condition A) is happening. All worksheet items, corresponding key entries and reference-note examples were read. Diagram/negation-sensitive answer pages were visually inspected. Original files were not edited. A [file manifest](source-audit-2026-10-11/manifest.json) records byte sizes, PDF page counts and SHA256 hashes. DOCX pagination is renderer-dependent and is not included in the 42-page total.\\r\\n\\r\\nDetailed audits:\\r\\n\\r\\n- [Intro training and translation: every-item ledger](2026-10-11-intro-translation-source-audit.md)\\r\\n- [If/then rephrasing and indicator identification: every-item ledger](2026-10-11-rephrase-indicator-source-audit.md)\\r\\n- [All three reference notes and worked examples](2026-10-11-reference-notes-source-audit.md)\\r\\n\\r\\n| Source set | What exists | Actual learner operation | Best role |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Intro training | 15 statements presented twice, 30 task instances; worksheet and key | Identify indicator and complete conditions; then diagram the same statements and form contrapositives | Scaffolded practice at two different stages |\\r\\n| Indicator ID | 13 identification items, 10 repeated statements for a further task, 20 additional items: 43 task instances | Identify indicators; label and diagram both roles; later write original and contrapositive in symbols and English | Early identification and later mixed checks, after key corrections |\\r\\n| If-Then rephrase | 25 items; worksheet and key | Express differently worded rules in if/then form | Bridge between identification and diagramming |\\r\\n| Translation, burrito | 35 statements; worksheet and key | Original diagram and contrapositive | Later sustained independent translation bank |\\r\\n| Reference notes | Three two-page PDFs | Group inventories; six-step worked process; contrapositive and four-case inference | Short references and staged worked demonstrations |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are **133 worksheet task instances**, excluding answer-key repetitions and note examples. Removing explicit within-set repeats gives **108 statement entries across the four sets**. This is not a claim of 108 globally novel scenarios or difficulty-equivalent questions: cross-set semantic deduplication and comparison with existing app items remain implementation work. Repeating a statement for a different operation is useful scaffolding, but is not a fresh transfer item.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How this fits the existing skeleton\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source notes describe: identify operator → identify both complete ideas → assign symbols → diagram → contrapositive → read back. The bootcamp should teach those operations in stages, not demand all six on first exposure.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Current course destination | What this source material can contribute | Keep out of this stage |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Sufficient / Necessary / Must arrow | Preserve the approved law-school anchor. Add only a compact source-backed clarification if a real gap remains. | New long indicator lectures and compulsory contrapositives |\\r\\n| Drill 1 | Selected clean recognition and condition-role questions from Intro and Indicator ID. Current drill already has 24 foundational checks, so substitute or create a separate assignable pool rather than append 133 items. | Full translation chains, negative compounds, premature symbol manipulation |\\r\\n| Find the complete condition | Warranty within 30 days; applicants who pass; students maintaining 4.0; members paying dues; unopened returns. Ask students to select the full condition or repair an omitted qualifier. | Repeating the complete indicator inventory |\\r\\n| Build the sufficient side | Clean Group 1 identification-to-if/then-to-arrow sequences. Start with provided phrases, then require the learner to identify them independently. | Counting a revealed diagram as demonstrated skill |\\r\\n| Necessary-condition words | Clean only-if/only-when/requires examples, permission versus action, only versus the only. Condense this page\'s remaining long introduction, selector and embedded recall in a future scoped revision. | Importing disputed group labels or treating must as proof of compliance |\\r\\n| Keep the whole meaning | Selected if/then rephrasing, compare equivalent formulations, repair omitted scope/modality/time. | A second full sufficient-condition introduction |\\r\\n| Conditional translation practice | Fresh mixed items requiring both full conditions and the direction without pre-highlighted answers; reasoning-specific feedback. | Reusing a just-worked sentence and calling it independent transfer |\\r\\n| Missing requirement / Contrapositive | Corrected four-case chart; Intro Part 2 and clean burrito items; negative-condition examples after negation is taught. | Negating compound conditions before teaching what the whole negation means |\\r\\n| Later independent / assignable practice | A cleaned, tagged bank with separate ordinary, negative, modal, temporal and compound-condition pools. | A raw PDF key imported as unquestioned ground truth |\\r\\n\\r\\nThe later whole-meaning lesson coordinates both groups and checks translations. The revised complete-condition lesson isolates selecting a full sufficient condition. Source items should reinforce that distinction instead of making both pages interchangeable.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Interaction designs justified by these worksheets\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Select the indicator, then both conditions.** Separate these selections so students cannot mistake a word such as always for a condition. Highlight only after an attempt.\\r\\n2. **Repair a cropped condition.** Show a draft that omits registered, applicant, before noon or can. Ask what must be restored and explain why the shorter claim changes meaning.\\r\\n3. **Rewrite before diagramming.** Student forms an if/then statement, then locates its two complete sides. Accept equivalent wording, not one exact string.\\r\\n4. **Place both sides.** Use the same accessible controls for pointer, keyboard and touch. Give feedback for reversal separately from feedback for missing words.\\r\\n5. **Distinguish permission from action.** For can attend, ask whether actual attendance follows. For must attend, distinguish required attendance from observed compliance.\\r\\n6. **Return to the same sentence later.** An earlier indicator item can become a contrapositive item after that lesson; label it as a new operation on a familiar rule, not a new source question.\\r\\n7. **Read the diagram back.** Check that the student\'s diagram preserves the original rule before adding a contrapositive. This follows the source\'s English-to-symbol-to-English sequence.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese are proposed adaptations, not features implemented by this audit.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Correct or hold before student use\\r\\n\\r\\nHighest-priority defects are documented per item in the linked ledgers:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Translation 20:** the key diagrams Sunny in Philadelphia → Always.
